--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page follows the title page of Friedrich Heinrich Jacobi's "On the Doctrine of Spinoza." It presents an epigraph from René Descartes, which serves as a philosophical warning about the dangers of relying solely on formal logic—a key theme in Jacobi's critique of Spinoza's geometric method of reasoning.</meta>

The Rules of Dialectics <note>original: "Dialecticorum Præcepta." In this context, dialectics refers to the formal logic and structured methods of debate used in traditional academic philosophy.</note> prescribe certain forms of debating which conclude so necessarily that reason—trusting in them—can still conclude something certain by the power of the logical form alone, even if it is somehow distracted from a clear and attentive consideration of the deduction <note>original: "illationis"</note> itself. Yet, we notice that truth often slips away from these chains, while those who use them remain entangled within them. This happens less frequently to others; indeed, we find through experience that even the most subtle fallacies <note>original: "sophismata"</note> hardly ever deceive anyone using a pure mind <note>original: "pura mente." This refers to the natural, unclouded capacity for human reason that functions without being forced into artificial structures.</note>, but instead usually deceive the sophists themselves.

->*Descartes.*<- <note>Latin: "Cartesius." This passage is taken from Rule X of Descartes' "Rules for the Direction of the Mind."</note>

<summary>This page features a Latin epigraph by René Descartes, translated into English, which critiques the rigid constraints of formal logic. Jacobi includes this to suggest that overly systematic reasoning (like Spinoza's) can actually obscure the truth rather than reveal it.</summary>
<keywords>René Descartes, Dialectics, Logic, Sophistry, Pure Mind, Epigraph, Jacobi, Rationalism</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page begins the "Vorbericht" (Preface) to Friedrich Heinrich Jacobi’s seminal work, "On the Doctrine of Spinoza in Letters to Mr. Moses Mendelssohn" (1785). While the previous page featured Goethe’s poem "The Divine," that poem was actually included as an attachment within this book. Here, Jacobi begins to explain the origins and structure of the publication, which ignited the famous "Pantheism Controversy" in German philosophy.</meta>

# Preface.

I have named my writing according to its occasion and the largest part of its contents, for even the letter to Hemsterhuis <note>François Hemsterhuis (1721–1790) was a Dutch philosopher whose work on aesthetics and "the moral sense" deeply influenced the German Romantics.</note> must be counted here, as an appendix <note>original: "Beylage," an archaic spelling of *Beilage*, meaning a supplement or enclosure.</note>, among those addressed to Mendelssohn <note>Moses Mendelssohn (1729–1786) was a preeminent Jewish philosopher of the Enlightenment. Jacobi’s private correspondence with him regarding the philosopher Spinoza was published here without Mendelssohn's full consent, causing a public scandal.</note>.

The fact that I also provide the history of these letters will be justified by that history itself.

I have briefly stated the intention of the work after the final letter, and thereafter, through to the end, I believe I have made it clearly enough known.

Furthermore, I have nothing to say for the time being to the attentive, — inquiring, — reader who is concerned solely with the **truth**. It happens against my

<summary>This preface introduces Jacobi’s collection of letters, explaining that the title reflects the book's contents, including correspondence with Moses Mendelssohn and François Hemsterhuis. Jacobi suggests the work's historical context will justify its publication and addresses readers seeking the truth.</summary>
<keywords>Friedrich Heinrich Jacobi, Moses Mendelssohn, François Hemsterhuis, Pantheism Controversy, German Philosophy, Enlightenment, Epistolary, Spinoza</keywords>

will, when another takes this writing into his hand. Let him demand nothing from me, just as I demand nothing from him. Pempelfort near Düsseldorf, August 28, 1785.

->Friedrich Heinr. Jacobi.<-

> All things are around the king of all: all things are for his sake: he is the cause of all beautiful things: around the second are the second things: around the third, the third. The human soul <note>The Latin *animus* can refer to the mind, spirit, or soul; here it represents the human intellect's striving for divine understanding.</note> strives to understand what those things are, looking upon those things that are related to itself: none of which are sufficient. But in the king himself, and in these things of which I have spoken, there is nothing of the sort: But what follows this, the soul declares. For what kind of question is this of yours, son of Dionysus and Doris? and how it is the cause of all evils? Rather, it is a certain stimulus <note>original: "stimulus," meaning a goad, prick, or inner drive that compels the mind to seek answers.</note> innate within our soul. Unless one roots this out, one will certainly never attain the truth.

->Plato to Dionysius. Epistle II.<-

<summary>This page concludes Jacobi’s preface with a firm statement of his independence from his readers' expectations, followed by a mystical quotation from Plato. The quote highlights the soul's innate drive to understand the "king of all" and the absolute nature of truth, which cannot be found in earthly things.</summary>
<keywords>Friedrich Heinrich Jacobi, Pempelfort, Plato, Second Epistle, Dionysius, metaphysics, pursuit of truth, Enlightenment philosophy</keywords>

*) Whoever blames me for including this poem—which as evidence here was hardly dispensable—with the caution I have exercised, must level even stronger reproaches against the translator of the two dialogues, *The Complaining Jupiter* and *The Ashamed Jupiter* <note>These refer to satirical works by Lucian of Samosata, a 2nd-century Greek writer who mocked the gods of Olympus.</note> in Lucian's writings. <meta>This page transitions from the text of Goethe’s "Prometheus" into the main narrative of the work. The previous page concluded the poem's defiant stance on human autonomy. This page begins the author’s account of the events and correspondence in 1783 that led to the publication of these ideas, specifically involving the circle of friends surrounding the late philosopher Gotthold Ephraim Lessing.</meta>

A close female friend <note>original: "Freundinn"; this refers to Elise Reimarus (1735–1805), a prominent intellectual and writer who acted as a bridge between various Enlightenment figures.</note> of Lessing <note>Gotthold Ephraim Lessing (1729–1781), a central figure of the German Enlightenment whose religious and philosophical views were highly debated after his death.</note>, who through him also became my own, wrote to me in February of the year eighty-three <note>1783.</note> that she was about to undertake a journey to Berlin, and asked me if I had any errands <note>original: "Aufträge"; likely referring to intellectual or social commissions rather than simple chores.</note> to be done there.

From Berlin, my friend wrote to me again. Her letter dealt primarily with Mendelssohn <note>Moses Mendelssohn (1729–1786), the preeminent Jewish philosopher of the Berlin Enlightenment and Lessing's closest friend.</note>, "this true admirer and friend of our Lessing." She informed me that she had spoken much with him about the immortalized one <note>original: "den Verewigten"; a common 18th-century honorific for the deceased, in this case, Lessing.</note>, and also about my humble self, and that he was about to [begin work] on his

<meta>This page continues the narrative from the previous page, where a mutual friend (Elise Reimarus) informed the author that Moses Mendelssohn was beginning a work on the late Gotthold Ephraim Lessing’s character and writings.</meta>

work on Lessing’s Character and Writings.

Various obstacles made it impossible for me to answer this letter immediately, and my friend’s stay in Berlin lasted only a few weeks.

Once she was back home, I wrote to her and inquired how much or how little Mendelssohn had become aware of Lessing’s religious views <note>original: "Gesinnungen"; referring to one's internal convictions or disposition.</note>. — Lessing had been a Spinozist <note>A follower of Baruch Spinoza’s philosophy (1632–1677). In the 18th century, "Spinozism" was a scandalous accusation, often equated with atheism or the belief that God and nature are the same thing.</note>.

To me, Lessing had expressed himself on this subject without any restraint; and since he was generally not inclined to conceal his opinions <note>original: "Meynungen"</note>, I might have assumed that what I knew of him had become known to several others. However, that he himself had never clearly explained this to Mendelssohn became known to me in the following way.

I had invited Lessing to accompany me to Berlin, and [received] the answer from him—

( 3 )

stay; we wanted to consider the matter together in Wolfenbüttel. When I arrived there, significant obstacles arose. Lessing wanted to persuade me to travel to Berlin without him, and he became more insistent every day. His primary motivation was Mendelssohn, whom he valued most highly among his friends. He earnestly desired that I should get to know him personally. In one such conversation, I once expressed my surprise that a man of such clear and sound intellect as Mendelssohn could have so zealously championed the proof of God’s existence from the mere idea <note>Referring to the "Ontological Proof," which argues that the very concept of a perfect being implies that being's existence.</note> [of God], as he had done in his *Treatise on Evidence* <note>A reference to Mendelssohn’s prize-winning 1763 essay, *Abhandlung über die Evidenz in metaphysischen Wissenschaften* (Treatise on Evidence in Metaphysical Sciences).</note>; and Lessing’s excuses led me directly to the question: Whether he had never asserted his own system of thought <note>original: "Lehrgebäude"; here referring to Lessing’s private, more radical philosophical framework.</note> against Mendelssohn.

"Never," answered Lessing . . . "Only once did I say to him roughly the same thing that struck you in *The Education of the Human Race* <note>Lessing's 1780 work, *Die Erziehung des Menschengeschlechts*, which suggested that religious dogmas were preliminary stages of a more rational, universal truth.</note> (§ 73). We did not reach a conclusion together, and I left it at that."

Various obstacles made it impossible for me to answer this letter immediately, and my friend’s <note>Likely referring to Elise Reimarus, a central figure in the intellectual circles of the time and a close confidante to both Lessing and Jacobi.</note> stay in Berlin lasted only a few weeks.

When she was back home, I wrote to her and inquired how much or how little Mendelssohn had learned of Lessing's religious views—specifically, that Lessing had been a <term>Spinozist</term> <note>A follower of Baruch Spinoza’s philosophy. In the 18th century, being called a Spinozist was often synonymous with being an atheist or a pantheist, as it suggested that God and Nature were one and the same, denying a personal, providential God.</note>.

Toward me, Lessing had expressed himself on this subject without any reservation; and since he was generally not inclined to conceal his opinions, I was permitted to assume that what I knew from him had become known to several others as well. However, that he himself had never clearly explained this to Mendelssohn became known to me in the following way.

His primary motivation was Mendelssohn <note>Moses Mendelssohn (1729–1786), a preeminent Jewish philosopher of the German Enlightenment and Lessing’s closest friend.</note>, whom he valued most highly among all his friends. He earnestly desired that I should get to know him personally. In one such conversation, I once expressed my astonishment that a man of such clear and correct understanding as Mendelssohn could have so zealously adopted the proof of the existence of God <note>original: "Daseyn Gottes"</note> from the mere idea [of Him], as he had done in his *Treatise on Evidence* <note>Referring to Mendelssohn's 1763 prize-winning essay, *Abhandlung über die Evidenz in metaphysischen Wissenschaften*, which defended the possibility of mathematical-style certainty in metaphysics.</note>; and Lessing's excuses led me directly to the question: Whether he had never asserted his own system of doctrine <note>Jacobi is referring to Lessing's private leanings toward Spinozism, which contradicted Mendelssohn's more traditional rationalist theism.</note> against Mendelssohn.

<meta>This page continues the text of Friedrich Heinrich Jacobi’s letter to Moses Mendelssohn, dated November 4, 1783. Jacobi is providing personal background to explain his philosophical temperament and the origins of his conversations with Lessing.</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>

I was still wearing a Polish coat <note>German: *polnischer Rock*; a long, belted garment common for young boys in the 18th century, signifying his early childhood.</note> when I already began to feel anxious about things of another world. My childish deep-mindedness <note>German: *Tiefsinn*; a term suggesting both profound contemplation and a tendency toward melancholy.</note> led me, in my eighth or ninth year, to certain strange—views (I do not know what else to call them) which cling to me to this very hour. The longing <note>German: *Sehnsucht*.</note> to reach certainty regarding the better expectations of mankind increased with the years, and it has become the main thread to which my remaining fates had to be tied. My original temperament and the education I received united to keep me in a reasonable mistrust of myself, and—for far too long—in an even greater expectation of what others could achieve. I went to Geneva, where I found excellent men who took me under their wing with generous love and true paternal loyalty. Later, I encountered other people, some of equal and some of even much greater reputation, who did not become the same to me for a long time, and into whose arms I often threw myself to my own great detriment. This gradually inclined me toward a certain

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Heinrich Jacobi’s letter to Moses Mendelssohn from page 8. Jacobi is describing how his early experiences and lack of self-confidence led him to value the insights of other "seekers of truth," eventually forming his own philosophical distinction between intuitive depth and analytical cleverness.</meta>

—trust in myself; I learned to gather my own strengths and to manage them with counsel <note>German: *zu Rathe halten*; meaning to husband one’s resources or use them wisely.</note>.

There are—as you know—only a few spirits who search for the truth out of an inner necessity; but to each among them, truth also revealed something of her inner life, so that none of them is so lowly that one could not listen to him with benefit. I discovered this trail; I followed it among the living and the dead; and I became ever more intimately aware that true depth of thought <term>Tiefsinn: A term for profound, meditative, or intuitive thinking that seeks the "core" of a matter.</term> has a common direction, like gravity in physical bodies. However, because this direction originates from various points on the periphery, it can no more produce parallel lines than it can produce lines that cross one another.

It is not the same with acuity of mind <term>Scharfsinn: Sharpness of intellect or analytical wit.</term>, which I would like to compare to the chords <note>Original: *Sennen*, an archaic spelling of *Sehnen*, referring to the geometric lines connecting two points on a curve.</note> of a circle, and which is often mistaken for depth of thought because it is "profound" regarding form and external matters. Here, the lines intersect as much as one wishes, and are sometimes even parallel to one another. A chord can run so close to the diameter that one might mistake it for the—

My journey came to pass, and on the afternoon of the fifth of July, I held Lessing in my arms for the first time.

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the conversation between Friedrich Heinrich Jacobi and Gotthold Ephraim Lessing in Wolfenbüttel, July 1780. On the previous page, Lessing had expressed his admiration for Goethe's "Prometheus" and his affinity for the concept of "One and All."</meta>

The following morning, when I had returned to my room after breakfast to get dressed, Lessing followed me in after a while. I was sitting while having my hair styled, <note>In the 18th century, a gentleman's morning routine often involved a lengthy process of styling hair or a wig by a servant.</note> and Lessing meanwhile settled himself quietly at a table at the end of the room. As soon as we were alone, and I had sat down on the other side of the table upon which Lessing was leaning, he began: "I have come to speak with you about my **One and All**. <note>original: ἐν καὶ πᾶν (*hen kai pan*). This Greek phrase is the central motto of Pantheism, suggesting that God and the universe are a single, unified whole.</note> You were startled yesterday."

**Me.** You surprised me, and I likely turned both red and pale, for I felt my own confusion. It was not terror. To be sure, I had expected nothing less than to find in you a **Spinozist** <note>A follower of Baruch Spinoza, whose philosophy was often equated with atheism in this period because it denied a personal, creator God.</note> or a **Pantheist**. And you told it to me so bluntly. I had come largely to obtain help from you *against* Spinoza.

**Lessing.** So you do know him then?

**Me.** I believe as well as very few have known him.

**Lessing.** Then you are beyond help. Better that you become his friend entirely. There is no other philosophy than the philosophy of Spinoza.

<summary>Lessing visits Jacobi the morning after their initial discussion to reaffirm his commitment to Pantheism, famously declaring that Spinoza's system is the only true philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>Gotthold Ephraim Lessing, Friedrich Heinrich Jacobi, Baruch Spinoza, Spinozism, Pantheism, One and All, Hen kai Pan, German Enlightenment</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the conversation between Friedrich Heinrich Jacobi and Gotthold Ephraim Lessing in Jacobi’s room. Jacobi is responding to Lessing’s previous claim that there is no other philosophy besides Spinoza’s.</meta>

**Me.** That may be true. For the **determinist**, if he wants to be consistent, must become a **fatalist** <note>A fatalist believes all events are predetermined and inevitable, leaving no room for human free will.</note>; then the rest follows of its own accord.

**Lessing.** I see we understand each other. All the more eager am I to hear from you what you consider the spirit of **<term>Spinozism</term>** <note>The philosophy of Baruch Spinoza, which suggests that God and Nature are the same thing (Pantheism).</note> to be; I mean the spirit that possessed Spinoza himself.

**Me.** It was likely none other than that ancient maxim: *nothing comes from nothing* <note>original: *a nihilo nihil fit*. This is a fundamental principle of ancient metaphysics: that matter cannot be created out of a void.</note>; which Spinoza, using more abstract concepts than the philosophizing **<term>Cabbalists</term>** <note>Followers of Kabbalah, a tradition of Jewish mysticism. Jacobi is linking Spinoza’s logic to older mystical ideas about the nature of God.</note> and others before him, took into consideration. Based on these more abstract concepts, he found that every instance of "becoming" within the Infinite—no matter what images one might disguise it in—would, through every change within it, imply that a "something" was being created out of nothing. He therefore rejected every transition from the Infinite to the finite; indeed, he rejected all *transient, secondary, or remote causes* <note>original: *Causas transitorias, secundarias oder remotas*. In traditional theology, these are the "middle steps" or external actions God takes to affect the world; Spinoza argued God is not a separate cause, but the world itself.</note>; and in place of an emanating being, he established a purely immanent **<term>Ensoph</term>** <note>From the Hebrew *Ein Sof*, meaning "without end." In Kabbalah, this refers to God in his most hidden, infinite state. Jacobi uses it here to describe Spinoza’s God as an "indwelling" force.</note>; an indwelling cause of the world, eternally unchanging in itself, which—taken together with all its consequences—would be one and the same.

<summary>Jacobi explains the core of Spinoza’s logic to Lessing, arguing that Spinoza rejected the idea of a creator God in favor of an "indwelling" cause where God and the universe are a single, unchanging entity.</summary>
<keywords>Friedrich Heinrich Jacobi, Gotthold Ephraim Lessing, Spinozism, Determinism, Fatalism, Kabbalah, Ensoph, Immanence, Metaphysics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Friedrich Heinrich Jacobi and Gotthold Ephraim Lessing. Jacobi is elaborating on the nature of Spinoza’s "Infinite Cause" (God) and why it differs from the traditional Christian or personal conception of a creator.</meta>

->.  .  .  .  .  .  .  .  .  *)<-

This immanent infinite **cause** <note>original: *Ursache*. In Spinoza’s philosophy, God is not a creator outside the world, but the "immanent cause"—meaning the cause exists *within* the universe itself.</note> has, as such, explicitly, neither **understanding** nor **will**, because, as a result of its **transcendental** <note>Something that goes beyond the limits of ordinary experience or individual things.</note> unity and pervasive absolute **infinity**, it can have no object of thought or of willing; and a faculty to produce a concept before the concept, or a concept that would exist before its object and be the complete cause of itself—as well as a **will** that...

*) I continue with this presentation, and condense what I can without writing down the interruptions, because I would otherwise have to be too long-winded. What follows immediately here was brought about when **Lessing** mentioned what he considered the most obscure point in **Spinoza**, which **Leibniz** also found to be so and did not fully understand (*Theodicy* §. 173.). <note>Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz was a major German philosopher; his *Theodicy* (1710) attempted to reconcile the existence of evil with a good God, and he frequently critiqued Spinoza’s more "mechanical" view of the divine.</note>

I make this reminder here once and for all, and I will not repeat it in the future when I take similar **liberties** <note>Jacobi is admitting that he is paraphrasing the conversation rather than providing a word-for-word transcript.</note>.

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the conversation between Friedrich Heinrich Jacobi (the narrator, "I") and the dramatist Gotthold Ephraim Lessing regarding the philosophy of Baruch Spinoza. The previous page established that Spinoza's God lacks personal intellect or will in the traditional sense, functioning instead as an all-encompassing substance.</meta>

<page-num>( 14 )</page-num>

I. That may be true. For the **determinist** <note>Someone who believes that every event, including human action, is decided by preceding causes.</note>, if he wants to be consistent, must become a **fatalist** <note>Someone who believes that all events are predetermined and inevitable, often implying a lack of personal agency.</note>: thereafter the rest follows of its own accord. *Lessing.* I notice we understand each other. All the more eager am I to hear from you what you consider the spirit of **Spinozism** to be; I mean that which had entered *Spinoza* himself. I. That was probably none other than the ancient adage: **nothing comes from nothing** <note>original: *a nihilo nihil fit*. This is a fundamental principle in ancient philosophy stating that matter or energy cannot be created out of a void.</note>; which *Spinoza* considered according to more abstract concepts than the philosophizing **Cabalists** <note>Followers of Kabbalah, a tradition of Jewish mysticism. Jacobi suggests Spinoza refined their older, more metaphorical ideas into a rigorous logical system.</note> and others before him. According to these more abstract concepts, he found that every instance of becoming within the **Infinite**, no matter what images one might use to cloak it, meant that through every change within it, a "something" was being created out of "nothing." He therefore rejected every transition from the **Infinite** to the **finite**; in general, all **transitory, secondary, or remote causes** <note>original: *Causas transitorias, secundarias oder remotas*. In traditional philosophy, these are causes that act from the outside or through a chain of events. Spinoza rejected these in favor of God being the immediate cause of everything.</note>; and in place of an emanating cause, he set a purely **immanent Ensoph** <note>Ensoph (or Ein Sof) is a Hebrew term from the Kabbalah meaning "the Infinite" or "without end." An "immanent" cause is one that stays within its effects rather than being separate from them.</note>; an indwelling, eternally unchanging cause of the world within itself, which—taken together with all its consequences—would be **One and the same**.

<summary>Jacobi argues that Spinozism is rooted in the principle that nothing comes from nothing, leading Spinoza to view God not as a creator outside the world, but as the infinite, indwelling "Ensoph" that is identical to the universe itself.</summary>
<keywords>Friedrich Heinrich Jacobi, Gotthold Ephraim Lessing, Spinozism, Determinism, Fatalism, Immanence, Ensoph, Kabbalah, Causality</keywords>

The objection that an **infinite series of effects** is impossible (they are not mere effects, because the inherent cause is always and everywhere) is self-refuting, because every series that is not to spring from nothing must absolutely be an infinite one. | :--- | :--- |
| **Metaphysics** | The study of the fundamental nature of reality and being. |

**I.** You are welcome to observe it from me. The whole matter consists in this: that I draw a conclusion from fatalism directly against fatalism, and against everything connected with it. — If there are only efficient causes <term>efficient causes: *würkende Ursachen*; the immediate mechanical forces that make something happen, like one billiard ball hitting another.</term> and no final causes <term>final causes: *Endursachen*; the ultimate purpose or "why" behind an action, such as an intention or goal.</term>, then the faculty of thought has nothing but a spectator's role in all of nature; its sole business is to accompany the mechanism of the acting forces. The conversation we are currently having is merely an affair of our bodies; and the entire content of this conversation, dissolved into its elements, is: extension, motion, degrees of velocity, along with the concepts of these, and the concepts of these concepts. The inventor of the clock did not, in essence, invent it; he only watched its emergence from blindly developing forces. Just so with Raphael when he designed the *School of Athens* <note>A famous Renaissance fresco by the artist Raphael depicting the great philosophers of antiquity.</note>; and Lessing, when he composed his *Nathan* <note>A reference to Lessing’s own masterpiece, the play *Nathan the Wise*.</note>. For even the affec— <note>The word is cut off: *Affekte* (affects or emotions).</note>

I will give you no peace; you must come out with this parallelism <term>parallelism: the philosophical idea that mind and body follow separate but perfectly synchronized paths without influencing each other</term> . . . people still always speak of Spinoza as if he were a dead dog . . . <note>Lessing’s famous remark suggests that while Spinoza was once feared, he was now being ignored or treated with contempt by the thinkers of the time.</note>

**I.** They would speak of him thus before as well as after. To grasp Spinoza, that requires a path too long and too hard—

---

...that its objective effects in the soul proceed according to the formal nature of the object itself; this is the same as what the ancients said, namely that true knowledge proceeds from cause to effects; except that they never, so far as I know, conceived—as we do here—of the soul acting according to certain laws, and like a kind of spiritual automaton <note>original Latin: "quòd ipsius effectus objectivi in anima procedunt ad rationem formalitatis ipsius objecti; id, quod idem est, quod veteres dixerunt, nempe veram scientiam procedere a causa ad effectus; nisi quod nunquam, quod sciam, conceperunt, uti nos hic, animam secundum certas leges agentem, & quasi aliquod automa spirituale." (Posthumous Works, p. 384.)</note>. The derivation of the word "automaton" <note>original Greek: αὐτόματον, meaning "acting of itself"</note>, and what Bilfinger <term>Bilfinger: Georg Bernhard Bilfinger, a philosopher who sought to harmonize the ideas of Leibniz and Wolff</term> recalls regarding it, is not unknown to me.

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the famous "Spinoza Conversation" between Friedrich Heinrich Jacobi (the narrator "I") and Gotthold Ephraim Lessing. ...turn one’s back on your philosophy, on all philosophy.

**I.** Why turn one’s back on all philosophy?

**Lessing.** Well, then you are a complete skeptic. <term>Skeptic: In a philosophical sense, someone who doubts that human reason is capable of reaching absolute truth or certain knowledge.</term>

**I.** On the contrary, I am withdrawing from a philosophy that makes complete skepticism necessary.

**Lessing.** And then you withdraw—where?

**I.** Toward the light, of which **Spinoza** says that it illuminates both itself and the darkness. <note>original German: "Dem Lichte nach... daß es ſich ſelbſt, und auch die Finſterniß erleuchtet." This is a reference to Spinoza’s *Ethics*, where he uses the metaphor of light to show that truth is its own standard: just as light reveals itself and the dark, truth reveals itself and the false.</note> — I love **Spinoza** because he, more than any other philosopher, has led me to the complete conviction that certain things cannot be explained <note>original: *entwickeln laſſen*, meaning to be unfolded or derived through logical steps</note>: things before which one must not close one’s eyes, but rather take them exactly as one finds them. I have no concept more intimate than that of final causes; <term>Final causes: Also known as teleology; the belief that things happen for a specific purpose or end-goal. Spinoza’s system generally rejected these, viewing the world as a chain of cause-and-effect instead.</term> no more living conviction than that I do what I think, rather than merely having to think what I do. Of course, I must then assume a source of thinking and acting that remains entirely inexplicable to me. But if I insist on explaining everything, then I must arrive at the second proposition, whose application to individual cases,

<summary>Jacobi explains his departure from rationalist philosophy, arguing that some truths—like human agency and purpose—must be accepted as they are found, even if they cannot be logically derived.</summary>

**I.** **Spinoza** also had to twist himself quite a bit to hide his **fatalism** <term>Fatalism: The belief that all events are predetermined and that human beings have no power to change their destinies or act freely.</term> when applying it to human behavior—especially in his fourth and fifth parts <note>A reference to the final two sections of Spinoza’s major work, the *Ethics*.</note>, where I would say he occasionally stoops to the level of a **sophist**. <term>Sophist: Here used to mean someone who uses clever but fallacious and misleading arguments to justify a point.</term> — And that was indeed what I asserted: that even the greatest mind must arrive at absurdities if he insists on explaining everything absolutely, making everything harmonize according to clear concepts, and allowing nothing else to stand as valid.

**Lessing.** And he who does not wish to explain?

**I.** He who does not wish to explain what is **incomprehensible**, but only wishes to know the boundary where it begins, and only to recognize that it is there: of him, I believe that he gains the most space for...

**Lessing.** Words, dear **Jacobi**, words! The boundary you want to set cannot be determined. And on the other side, you give a free and open field to **daydreaming**, <term>Daydreaming: The original German *Träumerey* in this context refers to "enthusiasm" or "fanaticism" (Schwärmerei)—a philosophical concern of the time that without strict reason, people would succumb to irrational mystical visions.</term> nonsense, and blindness.

**I.** I believe that boundary can be determined. I do not wish to set one, but rather only to find the one already set, and leave it be. And as for nonsense, **daydreaming**, and blindness . . .

**Lessing.** They are at home everywhere where confused concepts prevail.

**I.** More still, where **fabricated concepts** prevail. Even the blindest, most nonsensical faith—if not exactly the stupidest—has its high throne there. For anyone who has once fallen in love with certain explanations will blindly accept every consequence drawn from a conclusion they cannot refute, even if it meant he were **walking on his head**. <note>original: "daß er auf dem Kopfe gienge." <summary>In this segment of the dialogue, Jacobi and Lessing debate whether setting limits on human reason protects truth or merely opens the door to irrational fanaticism. ...ultimate purpose. Its ultimate purpose is that which cannot be explained: the indissoluble, the immediate, the simple.

. . . An immoderate passion for explanation <note>original: "Erklärungssucht," implying a compulsive or addictive drive to find a rational cause for every phenomenon.</note> makes us seek commonalities so heatedly that we disregard the differences; we always want only to connect and link things together, even though we might often separate them with far greater advantage . . . Furthermore, by only putting together and linking what is explainable in things, a certain illusion arises in the soul which blinds it more than it enlightens. We then sacrifice what *Spinoza*—profoundly and sublimely—calls the knowledge of the highest kind <term>Knowledge of the highest kind: In Spinoza’s *Ethics*, this refers to "intuitive knowledge" (*scientia intuitiva*), which understands things in their direct relationship to the essence of God, rather than through generalities or logic.</term> to the knowledge of the lower kinds; we close the eye of the soul, through which it perceives God and itself, so that we may look with the eyes of the body alone, without distraction . . .

**Lessing.** Good, very good! I can make use of all that too; but I cannot do the same thing with it. On the whole, your *Salto mortale* <term>Salto mortale: Latin for "death-leap" or "somersault." **Lessing.** Even that would require a leap, which I can no longer demand of my old legs and my heavy head.

To this conversation, of which I have provided only the essentials here, others followed, which brought us back to the same subjects by more than one path.

Once, Lessing said with a half-smile: that he himself might perhaps be the supreme being, and was currently in a state of extreme contraction. <term>Contraction: In the context of 18th-century philosophy and Kabbalah (specifically the concept of *Tzimtzum*), this refers to the idea of the infinite Divine "shrinking" or withdrawing itself to allow for the existence of the finite, material world.</term> — I pleaded for my own existence. — He replied that it was not exactly meant that way, and explained himself in a manner that reminded me of Henry More <note>original: "Heinrich Morus" (1614–1687), a Cambridge Platonist who blended mysticism with rational philosophy.</note> and van Helmont <note>Francis Mercurius van Helmont (1614–1698), a philosopher and chemist who sought to integrate Jewish mystical traditions into Christian thought.</note>. He did not consider a continuation of the human being after death, linked with personality, to be improbable. He told me that in Bonnet <note>Charles Bonnet (1720–1793) was a Swiss naturalist and philosopher known for his theories on the "ladder of being" and the immortality of the soul based on physical and spiritual development.</note>, whom he was just then rereading, he had encountered ideas that coincided exceptionally well with his own on this subject, and with his system in general. The course of the conversation, and my close acquaintance with Bonnet (all of whose writings I had formerly learned almost by heart), caused me to refrain from asking for further details about this. In order not to find this judgment paradoxical, one must be as familiar with Spinozism <note>Spinozism: The philosophy of Baruch Spinoza, often equated in the 18th century with atheism or pantheism—the belief that God and the universe are one and the same.</note> as Lessing was. What he called the exoteric shell of the *Aristaeus* can with every reason be viewed as a mere development of the doctrine of the inseparable, intimate, and eternal connection of the infinite with the finite; of the general (so far) indeterminate force with the determinate individual; and of the necessary contradiction in their directions—

With this, a large part of what Your Honor <note>The original German "Ew. Wohlgeb." <meta>This page continues the author’s defense of Gotthold Ephraim Lessing’s understanding of Spinoza. The author is refuting the suggestion that Spinoza’s earlier published works contradict his posthumous ones, and he begins to address the common 18th-century critique that Pierre Bayle had fundamentally misunderstood Spinoza’s system.</meta>

I cannot understand how you intend to set the *Theological-Political Treatise* <note>original: *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus* (1670), Spinoza's work on critique of religion and politics.</note> against them in any way. Whatever the *Theological-Political Treatise* contains of Spinoza’s system of doctrine <term>system of doctrine: or *Lehrgebäude*, referring to the internal architecture of a philosopher's complete set of ideas.</term>, his posthumous writings are in complete agreement with it. He also explicitly refers to that work in more than one place, right up until the end of his days.

You ask further: “Whether Lessing took the system as Bayle <note>Pierre Bayle (1647–1706), a French philosopher whose *Historical and Critical Dictionary* contained a highly influential, though critical, entry on Spinoza.</note> misunderstood it, or as others have explained it better.”

There is a difference between understanding and not misunderstanding. Bayle did not misunderstand Spinoza's system as far as the final conclusions are concerned; one can only say that he did not understand it deeply enough—tracing it back to its origins—nor did he perceive its grounds according to the author’s own intention. If Bayle, according to the spirit of your accusation, has misunderstood Spinoza, then by that same measure, Leibniz <note>Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz (1646–1716), a contemporary of Spinoza who struggled to reconcile his own philosophy with Spinoza’s perceived atheism.</note> misunderstood him even more severely. Compare, if you like, Bayle’s exposition in the first lines of Note N <note>A specific, famous section in Bayle’s dictionary entry on Spinoza that attempts to summarize the core of Spinozist thought.</note> with the

<summary>The author argues that Spinoza's various works are consistent with one another and defends Lessing's understanding of Spinoza against the common 18th-century interpretations provided by Bayle and Leibniz.</summary>
<keywords>Spinoza, Lessing, Pierre Bayle, Leibniz, Theological-Political Treatise, Posthumous Works, Note N, misunderstanding philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument regarding the interpretation of Spinoza, specifically comparing the critiques made by Leibniz and Bayle to those of other contemporary scholars. The author seeks to justify Lessing's perspective while addressing Moses Mendelssohn directly.</meta>

...what Leibniz states in sections 31 of the Preface to the *Theodicy* <note>original: *Præf. Theod.*, referring to Leibniz's *Essays on Theodicy* (1710), where he attempts to reconcile the existence of evil with a good God.</note>, and in sections 173, 374, and 393 of the *Theodicy* concerning the teachings of Spinoza. — If, however, Leibniz and Bayle did *not* misunderstand Spinoza’s system, then those others truly misunderstood it who thought they could explain it better; or, they distorted it. These latter ones are simply not my kind of people, and I will attest that they were not Lessing’s kind of people either.

The address: “Dear brother, the so heavily maligned Spinoza <note>original: *verschrieene Sp.*, referring to Spinoza's 18th-century reputation as a dangerous atheist or materialist.</note> may well, etc.” was not addressed to me by Lessing.

That I have presented my grievances so bluntly and dryly—indeed, perhaps somewhat harshly—you must not interpret as ill-intent, dear noble Mendelssohn <note>Moses Mendelssohn (1729–1786), the prominent Jewish Enlightenment philosopher who was then engaged in a heated public dispute (the *Pantheismusstreit*) over whether his late friend Lessing had been a Spinozist.</note>. Toward a man whom I revere just as you do, this tone was the only one that was fitting for me.

I am, etc. <note>original: *u. s. w.* (und so weiter); here used as a standard closing for a formal letter, such as "I remain your humble servant."</note>

Despite the somewhat too lively conclusion of this letter, the noble man <note>Moses Mendelssohn (1729–1786), a leading figure of the German Enlightenment.</note> to whom it was addressed received it with exceeding kindness, and even believed he still required my forgiveness. Seven months then passed without my hearing the least bit from Mendelssohn. Just at this time, a letter arrived from my female friend <note>This refers to Elise Reimarus (1735–1805), a literary figure and intellectual who acted as a key intermediary between Jacobi and Mendelssohn during this dispute.</note>, with the news: Mendelssohn was determined to set aside for now his work on *Lessing’s Character* <note>Mendelssohn intended to write a piece defending the character of his late friend Gotthold Ephraim Lessing, following Jacobi's shocking revelation that Lessing had been a secret follower of Spinoza.</note>, so that this summer—provided he had the health and leisure—he might first venture a bout with the **Spinozists**, or **"All-Oners,"** <note>German: *All Einern*. In the full joy of Mendelssohn’s decision, I replied by return mail; I then completed my letter to Hemsterhuis, and my head was now entirely clear and free of this whole business.

At the end of August, I traveled to Hofgeismar <note>A town in Hesse known at the time for its mineral springs and as a place for health retreats.</note> to restore my very weakened health and to once again enjoy life in the company of two of the greatest and most amiable people: the **Princess von Gallitzin** <note>Princess Amalia von Gallitzin (1748–1806), a prominent intellectual and leader of a Catholic Enlightenment circle in Münster.</note> and the **Minister von Fürstenberg** <note>Franz Friedrich Wilhelm von Fürstenberg (1729–1810), a leading statesman and educational reformer.</note>. In the 18th century, this often referred to critical remarks or objections in a scholarly debate.</note> against the philosophy contained in my writing. The packet had arrived at Düsseldorf immediately after my departure and had passed, open, through the hands of our mutual friend <note>Elise Reimarus, who acted as the conduit for their letters to avoid direct confrontation.</note>, who had provided it with a new cover.

Let us make it short, and begin with facts <term>Facts: Here, "Thatsachen" refers to empirical, observable realities rather than abstract metaphysical deductions.</term>.

<meta>This page continues the critique of Baruch Spinoza’s philosophy, transitioning from the previous page's military metaphors into a direct dialogue or "catechism" style debate. „ Do you agree that every action, whatever it may be, must have a direction? “

*Spinoza.* I do not agree. On the contrary, it appears evident to me that every original action <note>original: "action primitive." This refers to a fundamental or primary force that exists independently before interacting with other things.</note> can have only itself as its object and, consequently, cannot have a direction; what we call "direction" is never anything but the result of the effects of certain relationships <term>Relationships: translated from "rapports," referring to the way different entities or forces interact with one another to produce a specific path or outcome.</term>.

„ But is there a reason why everything... “

„ Do you admit that every single action must have a direction? “

*Spinoza.* No. On the contrary, it seems settled to me that every original action <note>original: "urſprüngliche Handlung." The German counterpart to the French "action primitive," implying a primordial act.</note> has only itself as its object, and therefore can have no direction; since what one calls direction is never anything else but the result of the effects of certain relationships.

**Sp.** I am far from denying all **freedom**, and I know that man has received his share of it. But this freedom does not consist in a chimerical <note>original: "chimérique." <column-break/>

**Sp.** I am far from denying all **freedom**, and I know that man has received his portion of it. But this freedom does not consist in a dreamed-of power of **being able to will**, because willing can only exist in the actually present, determined will. To attribute to a being a power of "being able to will" is exactly the same as if one were to attribute to it a power of "being able to exist," by virtue of which it would depend on the being to procure for itself actual existence. <note>The German text uses "Daſeyn" (Dasein), a foundational term in German philosophy meaning existence or "being-there."</note> The **freedom** of man is the essence of man—

<note>This concludes the thought that freedom is not an external choice, but the internal strength of a person's nature to act according to its own laws.</note>

<term>freedom / Freyheit / liberté</term>: Here redefined not as the ability to choose between two options, but as the internal power of a thing to exist and act according to its own nature.
<term>existence / Daſeyn / exiſtence</term>: The state of actually being present in reality, which the author argues cannot be created by a mere act of "willing."

<summary>In this passage, the character representing Spinoza redefines human freedom, arguing it is not an imaginary "power to will" but is instead synonymous with a person's inherent power and essence. He compares the traditional notion of free will to the impossible idea that a person could choose to bring themselves into existence.</summary>

on the part of a first and pure cause. I wrote to Mendelssohn that very instant to grant him the free use of my letters, and promised him without fail the specific reply he was still expecting by the coming month.

*Proof.* Whatever is determined to exist and act has been so determined by God <note>In Spinoza’s philosophy, "God" is synonymous with Nature or the totality of all that exists.</note>. But that which is finite and has a determined existence could not have been produced from the absolute nature of any attribute of God; for whatever follows from the absolute nature of any attribute of God is infinite and eternal. It must, therefore, follow from God or one of his attributes, insofar as it is considered as modified <note>original: "affectum"</note> in some way; for besides substance and modes <note>A "mode" (modus) is a specific expression or manifestation of substance, like a single thought or a single physical object.</note>, nothing exists, and modes are nothing but the modifications of God's attributes. It must therefore follow, or be determined to exist and act, by God or one of his attributes, insofar as it is modified by a modification that is finite and has a determined existence. This was the first point. Next, this cause again,

For the effects of God can neither be nor be conceived without their cause. It follows: II. That God cannot properly be called the **remote cause** <term>remote cause<note>original: "causa remota" — Traditionally, a cause separated from its effect by a chain of others; Spinoza argues here that because everything is "in" God, he is never truly distant from his effects.</note></term> of individual things, except perhaps for this reason: namely, to distinguish them from those things which he produced immediately, or rather, which follow from his absolute nature. For by a "remote cause" we understand one which is in no way joined with its effect. But all things that are, are in God, and so depend on God that without him they can neither be nor be conceived. *Posthumous Works* <note>original: "Opp. Posth." — Spinoza's *Opera Posthuma*, published in 1677, which contained his *Ethics*.</note> pages 25 & 26.

3) The following passages from **Kant** <note>Immanuel Kant (1724–1804) was a central figure in modern philosophy who sought to determine the limits of human understanding.</note>, which are entirely in the spirit of **Spinoza** <note>Baruch Spinoza (1632–1677) argued that everything in the universe is a part of one single substance, which he called God or Nature.</note>, may serve as clarification.

### VIII. What is prior in a thing according to its nature is not for that reason prior in time. Corporeal extension is, by nature,

possible through the limitation of a single underlying
time. Therefore, the original representation,
"Time," must be given as unlimited.
But where the parts themselves,
and every magnitude of an object, can only be
represented as determined through limitation,
there the whole representation must not be given
through concepts (for in that case the representations
of the parts would come first), but rather its
immediate **intuition** <term>intuition<note>original: "Anschauung" — In Kantian philosophy, this refers to the mind's direct, immediate representation of an individual object, as opposed to a "concept" which is a general or mediated idea.</note></term> must lie at the basis. **Critique
of Pure Reason** p. 32.

I wish to accompany these words with the following

> The intellect perceives certain things, or forms certain
> ideas absolutely, and others from other ideas. To be
> specific, it forms the idea of quantity absolutely, and
> does not attend to other thoughts; however, it does
> not form ideas of motion, except by attending to the
> idea of quantity. <note>original: "Intellectus quædam percipit, sive quasdam format ideas absolute, quædam ex aliis. > Those ideas which it forms absolutely express
> infinity; but it forms determinate ideas from
> others. For the idea of quantity, if it perceives
> it through a cause, then <note>original: "Quas absolute format, infinitatem exprimunt; at determinatas ex aliis format. Ideam enim quantitatis, si eam per causam percipit, tum" — Spinoza is explaining that fundamental ideas (like quantity) are naturally understood as infinite, whereas specific, limited things are understood through their relationship to other causes.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Spinoza’s "Treatise on the Emendation of the Intellect" (Tractatus de Intellectus Emendatione), specifically how the mind perceives quantity, motion, and eternity. ...after, before this or that mode of the same, even though they never exist for themselves without this or that definite mode—that is, according to time—

---

—determines quantity <note>original: "titatem determinat" — the first syllable "quan" is missing from the top of the page</note>; as when it perceives a body arising from the motion of a plane, a plane from the motion of a line, and finally a line from the motion of a point. These *perceptions do not serve for understanding, but only for determining quantity.* This is evident from the fact that we conceive of them as if they arose from motion, although motion is not perceived unless quantity is already perceived; and we can continue motion even into infinity, which we could by no means do if we did not have the idea of infinite quantity. <note>original Latin: "titatem determinat, ut cum ex motu alicujus plani corpus... si non haberemus ideam infinitæ quantitatis."</note>

The **intellect** <term>intellect<note>In Spinoza’s philosophy, the "intellect" represents the power of the mind to understand things clearly and rationally through their true causes, as opposed to the "imagination" which is based on sensory experience.</note></term> forms positive ideas before it forms negative ones.

It perceives things not so much under the aspect of duration, but under a certain **form of eternity** <term>form of eternity<note>original: "sub quadam specie æternitatis" — This is one of Spinoza’s most famous concepts. To see things "under the aspect of eternity" is to see them not as they appear in time and space, but as they follow necessarily from the eternal nature of God or the universe.</note></term>, and in infinite number; or rather, to perceive things, it attends neither to number nor to duration. However, when it **imagines** <term>imagines<note>Spinoza uses "imagination" to describe the mind's ability to form mental images based on the body's interactions with the world. Unlike the intellect, imagination sees things as finite, passing, and divided into parts.</note></term> things, it perceives them according to a certain number, a determinate duration, and quantity. (**On the Improvement of the Intellect**, *Posthumous Works*, pp. 390 & 391.)

I succumb to the temptation to transcribe one more passage from Spinoza’s **Metaphysical Thoughts** <note>original: "Cogitatis Metaphysicis" — a work by Spinoza published in 1663 as an appendix to his exposition of Descartes’ philosophy.</note>...

> Since we are accustomed to depict some images in our imagination of all things we understand, it happens that we imagine **non-beings positively**, as if they were **beings**. For the mind, viewed in itself alone, since it is a **thinking thing** <term>thinking thing<note>original Latin: "res cogitans" — A central term in early modern philosophy (notably Descartes and Spinoza) referring to the mind as an entity whose essence is the act of thinking.</note></term>, has no greater power for affirming than for denying. To **imagine**, however, is nothing other than to feel the traces found in the brain from the motion of the spirits <note>original Latin: "spirituum" — This refers to "animal spirits," which 17th-century thinkers believed were subtle fluids that traveled through the nerves to communicate between the senses and the brain.</note> which is excited in the senses by objects; such a **sensation can be nothing but a confused affirmation**. And hence it happens that we imagine all the modes which the mind uses for denying—such as **blindness**, **extremity** or an **end**, a **limit**, **darkness**, etc.—as if they were beings.
> 
> From this it clearly appears that these **modes of thinking** <note>original Latin: "modos cogitandi"</note> are not ideas of things, nor can they in any way be reduced to ideas; for which reason they also have no **object of the idea** <term>object of the idea<note>original Latin: "ideatum" — The external reality or actual object that corresponds to a mental concept.</note></term>, which...

<summary>This page explains Spinoza’s distinction between the intellect and the imagination, specifically how the mind mistakenly treats negative concepts like "darkness" or "limits" as if they were real, positive entities. It argues that imagination is merely a "confused affirmation" resulting from physical sensations in the brain.</summary>

It is, however, far different to inquire into the nature of things than into the modes by which things are perceived by us. Let us assume of the so-called four elements—water, earth, air, and fire—that all modes of **extension** <term>extension<note>original: "Ausdehnung" — In 17th and 18th-century philosophy, "extension" refers to the fundamental property of physical matter: the fact that it takes up space.</note></term> can be traced back to them and find their end in them. Now, bodily extension could be conceived in water without it being fire; in fire without it being earth; in earth without it being air, and so on. However, none of these modes would be conceivable in themselves without presupposing bodily extension; therefore, in each of these elements, according to its nature, extension would be the primary, the truly real, the substantial—the *nature naturing* <note>original: "*natura naturans*" — A Latin philosophical term used by Spinoza to describe God or Substance as the active, creative force of the universe, rather than the created results.</note>.

...be called unique. But in truth, if we wished to examine the matter more accurately, we could perhaps show that God is called "one" and "unique" only in an improper sense; however, the matter is not of such great importance—indeed, it is of no consequence to those who are concerned with things, and not truly with names. (*Metaphysical Thoughts*, Part I, chapter VI.) <note>original: "Cog. Metaph. P. I. c. VI." — Referring to Spinoza's *Cogitata Metaphysica*, a work where he explores Cartesian philosophy and his own early metaphysical ideas.</note> .... Regarding the demonstration which I establish in the Appendix of Geometrical Demonstrations for Descartes' *Principles of Philosophy*, namely that God can only be called "one" or "unique" very improperly; I respond that a thing is called "one" or "unique" only with respect to its **existence** <term>existence<note>original: "existentiæ" — The actual state of being or occurring in reality.</note></term>, but not truly with respect to its **essence** <term>essence<note>original: "eſſentiæ" — The fundamental nature or definition that makes a thing what it is.</note></term>. For we do not conceive of things in terms of numbers unless they have first been reduced to a common genus <note>A "genus" is a general category or class of things that share similar characteristics.</note>. Someone who, for example, holds a **sesterce** <note>original: "seſtertium" — an ancient Roman coin.</note> and a **thaler** <note>original: "imperialem" — the "Reichsthaler," a standard silver coin used in the Holy Roman Empire.</note> in their hand will not think of the number two unless they can call this sesterce and thaler by one and the same name—namely, numbers or coins. For then they can affirm that they have two coins or pieces of money, because they designate not only the sesterce but also the thaler by the name of a coin or money. From this, it is clearly evident that no thing is named "one" or "unique" unless another thing has first been conceived which (as has been said) agrees with it.

<summary>This page presents Spinoza's argument that God is "one" only in a loose sense, as numbers are human mental constructs used to group similar objects rather than descriptions of God's unique essence.</summary>

## XII. *Determination is negation, or determination does not belong to a thing according to its being* 5) <note>original: "Determinatio est negatio, seu determinatio ad rem juxta suum esse non pertinet" — This is a central tenet of Spinoza's philosophy: to define something by its specific traits (determination) is to state what it is *not* (negation), which cannot apply to an infinite, all-encompassing God.</note>. Individual things, therefore, insofar as they exist only in a certain, determined way, are **non-beings** <note>original: "non-entia"</note>; and the undetermined, infinite Being is the only true **real being, that is, it is all being, and besides which no being is given** 6) <note>original: "ens reale, hoc est, est omne esse, & praeter quod nullum datur esse"</note>.

## XIII. So that the matter might become even clearer,

---

> Since, in truth, God's **existence** is his **essence**, and we cannot form a universal idea of his essence, it is certain that he who calls God "one" or "unique" has no true idea of God, or speaks of him improperly. (*Letter 50, Posthumous Works, p. 557.*) <note>original: "Ep. L. Opp. Posth. p. 557." — referring to Spinoza's correspondence.</note>

### XIV.

According to Spinoza, an infinite **extension** <term>extension<note>original: "Ausdehnung" — in 17th-century philosophy, this refers to the physical world or matter, which occupies space.</note></term> and an infinite **thinking** are attributes of God. Both together constitute only **One** inseparable being 7), so that it is a matter of indifference under which of these two attributes one views God, because the order and connection of ideas is identical to the order and connection of things; and everything that follows from the infinite nature of God **formally** <note>original: "formaliter" — this refers to how things exist in reality as actual, physical objects.</note> must also follow from it **objectively** <note>original: "objectivè" — this refers to how things exist as objects of thought or ideas in the mind.</note>, and vice versa 8).

13) *Ethics, Part I, Scholium to Proposition 29.* <note>original Latin: "Per Naturam naturantem nobis intelligendum est id, quod in se est, & per se concipitur, sive talia substantiæ attributa, quæ æternam, & infinitam essentiam exprimunt, hoc est, Deus, quatenus ut causa libera, consideratur. Per naturatam autem intelligo id omne, quod ex necessitate Dei naturæ, sive uniuscujusque Dei attributorum sequitur, hoc est, omnes Dei attributorum modos, quatenus confiderantur, ut res, quæ in Deo sunt, & quæ sine Deo nec esse, nec concipi possunt."</note> By "Nature naturing" we must understand that which is in itself and is conceived through itself, or such attributes of substance as express an eternal and infinite essence—that is, God, insofar as he is considered as a free cause. By "Nature natured," however, I understand everything that follows from the necessity of God's nature, or from any of God's attributes—that is, all the modes of God's attributes, insofar as they are considered as things which are in God, and which without God can neither exist nor be conceived. *Posthumous Works p. 27.*

14) *Ethics, Part I, Proposition 31.* <note>original Latin: "Intellectus actu, sive is finitus sit, sive infinitus, ut & voluntas, cupiditas, amor &c. ad naturam naturatam; non vero ad naturantem referri debent."</note> The actual intellect, whether it be finite or infinite, as well as will, desire, love, etc., must be referred to <term>Natura naturata<note>Latin for "Nature natured." *Demonstration.* For by "intellect," we do not mean absolute thinking, but only a certain mode of thinking, which differs from other modes, namely desire, love, etc. Therefore, it must be conceived through absolute thinking—specifically, through some attribute of God which expresses the eternal and infinite essence of thought. It must be conceived in such a way that without this attribute, it can neither exist nor be conceived. For this reason, it must be referred to *Natura naturata*, and not to *Natura naturans*, as must the other modes of thinking. Which was to be demonstrated.

*Scholium.* The reason why I speak here of the <term>actual intellect<note>original: "intellectus actu". XXVI. Since we have only the single representation of physical <term>extension</term> <note>In philosophy, "extension" refers to the property of taking up space, which characterizes the material world</note> as a property of <term>substance</term> <note>The underlying, fundamental reality that exists by itself</note>, aside from thinking, we also restrict ourselves to it alone and say: because consciousness is inseparably linked with extension, everything that occurs in extension must also occur in consciousness.

---

No cognitions can take place in us, nor any connection and unity among them, without that unity of consciousness which precedes all given data <note>original Latin: *datis*</note> of <term>intuitions</term> <note>The immediate, non-intellectual way our mind receives sensory information</note>, and in relation to which all representation of objects is alone possible. This pure, original, immutable consciousness, I will now call *transcendental apperception* <note>A Kantian term for the "I think" that accompanies all our thoughts and allows us to organize them into a single, unified experience</note>. That it deserves this name is already evident from this: that even the purest objective unity, namely that of concepts *prior to experience* <note>original Latin: *a priori*</note> (*space and time*), is only possible through the relation of intuitions to it. The numerical unity of this *apperception* thus lies *prior to experience* <note>original Latin: *a priori*</note> at the very basis of all concepts, just as the *manifold* <note>The variety or "multiplicity" of raw sensory data</note> of *space* and *time* lies at the basis of the intuitions of *sensibility*.

*Critique of Pure Reason, p. 107.*

XXVII. The consciousness of a thing we call its **concept** <note>The German *Begriff* refers to the mental grasp or intellectual understanding of a thing</note>, and this concept can only be an **immediate** concept.

XXVIII. An immediate concept, considered in and for itself alone, is without **representation** <note>The German *Vorstellung* refers to a mental image or a "presentation" of something before the mind's eye. The author argues that a direct, pure concept doesn't need a mental picture</note>.

XXIX. ## XXXIII.

The immediate concept <note>original German: *Begriff*. In Spinoza’s Latin, this is *idea*. The author is describing the "idea of the mind" (*idea mentis*).</note> of the immediate concept of the body constitutes the consciousness of the soul, and this consciousness is united with the soul in the same way as the soul is united with the body. Namely: the soul's consciousness expresses a certain definite form of a concept, just as the concept itself expresses a certain definite form of an individual thing 20). However, the individual thing, its concept, and the concept of this concept are entirely one and the same thing <note>original Latin: *unum & idem*</note>, which is only viewed under different attributes and properties 21).

26) I must remind the reader once more—because it is of infinite importance in Spinoza's system—that, apart from absolute thought (which is the very first in concept and is without any representation), every other thought must refer to the immediate concept of a truly existing individual thing and its properties, and can be given in that thing alone. Thus, it is absolutely impossible for any kind of concept of things to exist before individual things are actually present. However, individual things have existed from eternity, and God has never existed "before" them in any way other than the way he still exists before them now, and will exist before them for all eternity: namely, solely in terms of nature or essence <term>essence: the fundamental "what-ness" of a thing, as opposed to its physical existence in time</term>.

<summary>This page explains Spinoza’s rule that finite things and their ideas do not spring directly from God’s infinite nature, but follow a chain of other finite causes. It clarifies that while God is logically "prior" to the world, individual things and the mind's ideas of them have existed eternally alongside God's essence.</summary>

XXXVI. Individual things arise mediately <note>original: *mittelbar* — meaning they are produced through a chain of intermediate causes rather than directly from God’s infinite nature.</note> from the Infinite; or they are brought forth by God by virtue of the immediate affections <term>affections: also known as "modes," these are the specific ways or states in which God's attributes, like thought or extension, are expressed.</term> or qualities of his essence. These, however, are equally eternal and infinite with God, and he is their cause in the same way that he is the cause of himself. Individual things, therefore, arise from God (immediately) only in an eternal and infinite manner, not in a fleeting, finite, and transient way; for in that latter sense, they arise merely one from another, as they mutually produce and destroy each other, yet nevertheless persist unchangeably in their eternal existence.

XXXVII. The same applies to the concepts <note>original: *Begriffen* — referring to the mental representations or "ideas" that correspond to physical things.</note> of individual things; namely, they are

<summary>This page explains that while individual things seem finite and fleeting, they arise from God's eternal essence and exist as part of an unchanging, infinite system of causes. It establishes that the same rules governing the existence of physical objects also apply to the concepts or ideas of those objects.</summary>

**27)** <note>original Latin: *Eth. P. II. Prop. VIII.*</note> *Ethics, Part II, Proposition 8.* The ideas of individual things, or of non-existing modes <term>modes: the specific, finite expressions of God's infinite attributes, such as a single thought or a single physical object.</term>, must be contained in God’s infinite idea in the same way that the formal essences <term>formal essence: the actual nature or reality of a thing as it exists within God’s attributes, even if it is not currently "alive" or "present" in time.</term> of individual things, or modes, are contained in God’s attributes.

*Demonstration.* <note>original Latin: *Ex Axiomate IV. P. I. Effectus cognitio a cognitione causæ dependet & eandem involvit.*</note> From Axiom 4, Part I: Knowledge of an effect depends on the knowledge of its cause and involves the same.

*Corollary.* <note>original Latin: *Hinc sequitur, quod, quamdiu res singulares non existunt...*</note> From this it follows that, as long as individual things do not exist except insofar as they are contained in God’s attributes, their objective being <term>objective being: the reality of a thing as it exists as a concept or "object" of thought in the mind.</term>—or their ideas—do not exist except insofar as God’s infinite idea exists. And when individual things are said to exist, not only insofar as they are contained in God’s attributes, but also insofar as they are said to have duration <note>original: *durare* — referring to existence within time.</note>, their ideas will also involve the existence through which they are said to have duration.

XXXVIII. Therefore, no concept of an individual, actually existing, or thoroughly determined thing can be present in God insofar as He is infinite; rather, it is present in Him and produced by Him only as a

---

*Scholium.* <note>original Latin: *Si quis ad uberiorem hujus rei explicationem exemplum desideret...*</note> If anyone should desire an example for a fuller explanation of this matter, I could certainly provide none that would adequately explain the thing of which I speak here—since it is unique—nevertheless, I will try to illustrate the matter as best as I can. Namely, a circle is of such a nature that for all straight lines intersecting each other within it, the rectangles formed by their segments <term>rectangles under the segments: a geometric theorem (the Intersecting Chords Theorem) stating that when two chords intersect, the product of the segments of one chord equals the product of the segments of the other.</term> are equal to one another; therefore, an infinite number of equal rectangles are contained within a circle. However, none of them can be said to exist except insofar as the circle exists, nor can the idea of any of these rectangles be said to exist except insofar as the idea of the circle is comprehended. Now, imagine that out of those infinite rectangles, only two—namely D and E—actually exist. Truly, their ideas also now exist not only insofar as they are merely comprehended in the idea of the circle, but also insofar as they involve the existence of those specific rectangles; because of this, they are distinguished from the ideas of the other remaining rectangles. *Posthumous Works, page 47.* <note>This refers to the *Opera Posthuma* of Baruch Spinoza, published in 1677, specifically Part II, Proposition 8 of the *Ethics*.</note>

<summary>This page argues that individual concepts exist in God only as part of His infinite nature and uses a geometric analogy of rectangles within a circle to show how finite parts relate to an infinite whole.</summary>

28) *Ethics, Part II, Proposition 9.* <note>original Latin: *Eth. P. II. Prop. 9.*</note> The idea of an individual thing, existing in actuality, has God as its cause, not insofar as He is infinite; but insofar as He is considered to be affected by another idea of an individual thing existing in actuality, of which God is also the cause, insofar as He is affected by a third idea, and so on to infinity.

*Demonstration.* <note>original Latin: *Demonstr.*</note> The idea of an individual thing, existing in actuality, is a singular mode <term>mode: in Spinoza's philosophy, a "mode" is a specific manifestation or "state" of God's substance—a finite thing like a single thought or a physical object.</term> of thinking, and is distinct from the others; therefore it has God, insofar as He is merely a thinking thing, as its cause. But not insofar as He is an absolutely thinking thing; rather, insofar as He is considered to be affected by another mode of thinking, and of this also, insofar as He is affected by another, and so on to infinity. But the order and connection of ideas is the same as the order and connection of causes; therefore, the cause of one singular idea is another idea, or God, insofar as He is considered to be affected by another idea, and of this also, insofar as He is affected by another, and so on to infinity. Which was to be demonstrated. <note>original: Q. E. D. (*Quod Erat Demonstrandum*)</note>

<summary>This page concludes a discussion on the nature of finite ideas in God, asserting they only exist in relation to their physical counterparts. XXXIX. All individual things mutually presuppose one another and relate to one another, so that one of them without all the others, and all the others without this one, can neither exist nor be conceived; that is, together they constitute an inseparable whole; or more correctly and strictly: they are present and together in an absolutely indivisible, infinite thing, and in no other way 29).

---

*Corollary.* <note>original Latin: *Coroll.*</note> Whatever happens in the singular object of any idea, there exists a knowledge of it in God, insofar as He has only the idea of that same object.

*Demonstration.* <note>original Latin: *Demonstr.*</note> Whatever happens in the object of any idea, an idea of it exists in God, not insofar as He is infinite, but insofar as He is considered as affected by another idea of a singular thing; but the order and connection of ideas is the same as the order and connection of things; therefore, the knowledge of that which happens in some singular object will be in God, insofar as He only has the idea of that same object. *Which was to be demonstrated.* <note>original Latin abbreviation: *Q. E. D.*</note>

### XL. The absolutely indivisible being, in which bodies exist and are together, is infinite absolute extension.

Regarding this important point, one should consult Propositions 12 and 13 in the first part of the *Ethics*, and especially the Scholium <note>Scholium: a marginal note or explanatory commentary used by Spinoza to provide deeper context to his logical proofs</note> to Proposition 15. Furthermore, see the remarkable letter *on the infinite* <note>original Latin: *de infinito*</note> to *L. Mayer* <note>Lodewijk Meyer, a close friend and editor of Spinoza</note>, *Posthumous Works* <note>original: *Opp. Posth.* (*Opera Posthuma*)</note>, p. 465; and the no less remarkable letter to *Oldenburg* <note>Henry Oldenburg, Secretary of the Royal Society</note> *on the whole and the part* <note>original Latin: *de toto & parte*</note>, ibid. p. 439. Likewise, letters 39, 40, and 41 to an unnamed person, *Posthumous Works*, pp. 519–527.

It is scarcely comprehensible how people could have accused *Spinoza* of suggesting that the unlimited arises from the gathering together of the limited, or that his infinite substance was merely an absurd aggregate of finite things—and consequently, that its empty unity was a mere *abstraction* <note>original Latin: *Abstractum*; something existing only as an idea rather than a reality</note>. I say, it is scarcely comprehensible how such things could be attributed to *Spinoza*, since his entire system proceeds from exactly the opposite view, and this opposite view is its very soul. Moreover, among all philosophers, no one was more careful than he to guard himself against...

<summary>This page defines the indivisible foundation of the physical world as "infinite absolute extension" and defends Spinoza against the critique that his concept of God or Substance is simply a collection of finite parts.</summary>

<keywords>Spinoza, Ethics, extension, substance, infinite, finite, Lodewijk Meyer, Henry Oldenburg, metaphysics</keywords>
