### XLI. The absolutely indivisible being, in which all concepts exist and are found together, is infinite absolute thought.

### XLII. Both <note>Referring to "extension" and "thought," the two attributes of God discussed here and on the previous page.</note> belong to the essence of God and are contained within the same. Therefore, God can no more be called an extended physical thing than a thinking thing *distinctively* <note>original Latin: *distinctivè*; meaning to name one attribute while excluding the other</note>; rather, the very same substance is extended and, at the same time, also thinking. Or, to put it in other words: no separate, distinct reality underlies any of the attributes of God in such a way that they exist as things apart from one another, each of which for itself [is] its own—

---

—to view or pass off what is merely a *mode of thinking* <note>original Latin: *modus cogitandi*; in Spinoza's system, this is a specific expression or "version" of the attribute of thought</note>, or mere *beings of reason* <note>original Latin: *entia rationis*; concepts that exist only in the mind to help us understand things, rather than things with their own independent physical reality</note>, as something real. “The whole must necessarily be prior to the part” <note>original Latin: *Totum parte prius esse necesse est*</note> — this was already a general principle of Aristotle, which this royal thinker knew how to correctly apply even to the "improper" whole of a commonwealth <note>Referencing Aristotle's *Politics*, Book I, Chapter 2, where he argues the state is naturally prior to the individual</note>. Spinoza follows this sublime and fruitful principle everywhere.

31) <term>Ethics</term> <note>Referring to Benedict de Spinoza’s *Ethics*</note> Part II, Propositions 45, 46, and 47, along with their Scholia <note>original: *cum suis Scholiis*; "Scholia" are explanatory remarks or "asides" Spinoza added to clarify his formal proofs</note>; to be compared with the 3rd and 4th propositions of this same part, and the 30th and 31st of the first.

It is necessary here to recall the proof so often repeated by Spinoza: that the essence of a thing does not include "number" within itself <note>Spinoza argued that essence is a quality of being, not a quantity; therefore, the fundamental nature of something cannot be "multiplied" or counted in the way physical objects are</note>, and that multiple things—in that which they have in common—cannot be viewed as multiple things, but rather, to that extent, only as parts of a single thing.

Upon this very foundation he built his ingenious and truly sublime theory of true ideas, of "common" and "complete" concepts <note>Spinoza’s *notiones communes* (common notions) are universal truths that are equally present in the part and the whole, forming the basis of rational thought</note>, of certainty, and of the human understanding in general.

Thus we have a revelation of nature, which not only commands but compels each and every human being to believe, and through that faith to accept eternal truths.

The religion of Christians teaches another kind of faith—it does not command it. It is a faith that has as its object not eternal truths, but rather the finite, contingent nature of man. It instructs man on how he may adopt certain qualities through which he might gain progress in his existence; so that he may soar upward to a higher life, and with that to a higher consciousness, and within that, to a higher knowledge. ## II.

Kabbalistic philosophy <note>A Jewish mystical tradition that Jacobi, following other scholars of his time, attempted to analyze through a rationalist philosophical lens.</note>, insofar as it lies open to investigation, and according to its best commentators—von Helmont the Younger *) and Wachter **)—is, as a philosophy, nothing other than undeveloped or newly muddled Spinozism.

---

religion. <note>This completes the sentence from the previous page: "...the correctly understood doctrine of Spinoza allows for no kind of religion."</note> A certain froth of Spinozism, on the other hand, is quite compatible with all kinds of superstition and fanaticism <note>original: "Schwärmerey," a derogatory term used by Enlightenment thinkers for irrational religious enthusiasm</note>, and one can blow the most beautiful bubbles with it. The resolute atheist <note>original: "Gottesläugner," literally "God-denier"</note> should not hide under this froth; the others must not deceive themselves with it.

*) The younger von Helmont <note>Franciscus Mercurius van Helmont (1614–1698), a Flemish philosopher and diplomat known for his work on the Kabbalah.</note> is at least the editor of the work published in Amsterdam in the year 1690 under the following title: *Small Philosophical Works, which contain the Principles of the Most Ancient and Most Recent Philosophy; and Common Philosophy Refuted, etc.* <note>original: "Opuscula Philosophica, quibus continentur Principia Philosophiæ Antiquissimæ & Recentissimæ; ac Philosophia Vulgaris Refutata &c."</note>

**) *The Kabbalistic Enlightener, or a Brief and Succinct Review of the Hidden Philosophy of the Hebrews. Compiled by Johann Georg Wachter. Rome. 1706.* <note>original: "Elucidarius Cabalisticus, sive Reconditæ Hebræorum Philosophiæ Brevis & Succincta Recensio. Epitomatore Joh. Georgio Wachtero. Romæ. 1706." Wachter was a significant scholar who argued for a link between Spinozism and Jewish mysticism.</note>

--- Page 212 ---
<meta>This page continues Friedrich Heinrich Jacobi’s presentation of his core philosophical propositions. Following his discussion of the Kabbalah and Spinozism, he now addresses the dominant German philosophy of his time (Leibniz-Wolffianism) and the limitations of logical demonstration.</meta>

### III.

The Leibniz-Wolffian philosophy <note>The dominant philosophical system in 18th-century Germany, which combined the metaphysical ideas of Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz with the systematic, logical framework of Christian Wolff.</note> is no less fatalistic <note>In this context, fatalism refers to a strictly deterministic world-view where all events are necessary and human free will is considered an illusion.</note> than the Spinozist one, and leads the persistent researcher back to the principles of the latter.

### IV.

Every path of demonstration <note>Referring to formal, logical, or mathematical proof.</note> ends in fatalism.

### V.

We can only demonstrate similarities; and every proof presupposes something already proven, the primary principle <note>original: "Prinzipium"</note> of which is revelation.

### VI.

The element of all human knowledge <note>original: "Erkenntniß"</note> and efficacy is faith *).

---

*) "Who can prove that in a historical or poetic painting, this or that line is from the master who signed his name to it, or whose style is unmistakable? Who can prove that a letter you receive from a known or unknown hand—was written by a single person? — All of this, however...

<summary>In these propositions, Jacobi argues that all rationalist systems of proof inevitably lead to fatalism (determinism) and asserts that the true foundation of human knowledge is not logic, but faith and revelation.</summary>
<keywords>Leibniz-Wolffian philosophy, Fatalism, Spinozism, Demonstration, Revelation, Knowledge, Faith, Logic</keywords>

One of my friends had written to me at the beginning of June regarding the work that was occupying Mendelssohn, and reported that he had been told from Berlin that it would bear the title: *Morning Thoughts on God and Creation*. > ...your feeling, your sense of intuition, or something within you will tell you—which in our philosophies and theologies as yet has no name—and which at every moment in all people acts a thousand times faster and a thousand times more effectively than all the philosophies and theologies in the world—And this something, which guides, drives, draws back, warns, admonishes, and determines you in the gentlest and most powerful way— . . . At the very least, it would become clear why certain things seemed so utterly incomprehensible to him and why they increasingly eluded his understanding the more I endeavored to provide him with explanations.

Upon this news, I locked up my papers again until I had seen the work of my famous opponent, as mine could no longer appear simultaneously with his. I made arrangements to obtain it as quickly as possible.

In the meantime, a letter arrived from *Mendelssohn*, sent open within an empty envelope from our mutual friend <note>This refers to Elise Reimarus, who acted as a mediator between Jacobi and Mendelssohn during the "Pantheism Controversy."</note>. It was not the answer I had looked forward to for so long; not a syllable referred to it. Our mutual friend and another friend were his witnesses that, in accordance with his current weakness <note>Mendelssohn suffered from chronic poor health and physical frailty, which he often cited as a reason for delays in his work.</note>, he had not been idle in our legal matter <note>original: *Streitsache*; used here metaphorically to describe their formal philosophical dispute.</note>; and if fate <note>The OCR shows "Ein ***," likely representing a humble reference to God or Providence.</note> did not completely reject his work, the next **Fair Catalogue** <note>original: *Meßkatalogus*; the official bibliography of new books published for the major trade fairs in Leipzig and Frankfurt.</note> would confirm that testimony. I return to the propositions set out earlier, regarding which I must still remark that I am by no means inclined to post them as theses <note>A reference to the practice of posting "theses" for public debate, most famously done by Martin Luther.</note> and defend them against every attack. Even in the realm of truth, little is seldom gained through war; the faithful diligence of each in his own sphere, and a voluntary, honest exchange, would also be the most beneficial and best here. Why this ill-tempered zeal against a lack of knowledge? — Instead of exposing it and punishing with scorn those—

--- Page 220 ---
<meta>This page completes the sentence from the previous page, where Jacobi was discussing how one should react to a "lack of knowledge" in others. He transitions into a metaphorical discussion on the nature of truth versus error using the imagery of light and darkness.</meta>

<page-num>( 178 )</page-num>

—this deficiency <note>Completing the German word "diesen" from the previous page.</note> that annoys you: Remedy it through giving! Through the act of giving, you will show yourself as the one who has more, and prove it to the one in need. Truth is clarity, and relates everywhere to reality, to facts <note>original: "Facta," Latin for deeds or established facts.</note>. Just as it is impossible for objects to become visible to a blind person—as long as they remain blind—through any kind of art, it is likewise impossible for a sighted person not to perceive them in the light and distinguish them for himself. But we demand of error, as if it were the truth, that it see itself and recognize itself; and we are afraid of it, as if it were as strong as the truth. Can darkness really penetrate the light and extinguish its rays? Into the darkness, on the other hand, the light penetrates and makes it manifest by partially illuminating it. And just as day is created only by the sun, so night is created only by the setting of the sun.

To be sure, anyone can make their own narrow dwelling as dark as night, even at midday. Into his narrow darkness he can then bring brightness again: but no brightness...

<meta>This page continues the metaphor from page 178, contrasting the natural, divine light of the sun (Truth) with the artificial, fragile light (Error or human artifice) produced by people in their narrow dwellings.</meta>

...as if from heaven. The frail flame will be extinguished by a mere accident—perhaps even by the very hand that wished to tend it. And even if it persists for a fleeting time, it will certainly sicken the eye in the long run. 

Where foul soil <note>The author uses "soil" (Boden) as a metaphor for the underlying intellectual or moral state of a society or individual.</note> stretches across vast regions, the rising, sluggish, and cold vapors ward off the sun; as a result, the soil becomes ever worse, and the murky, poisonous clouds grow ever more numerous. Artificial fire or heavy projectiles <note>This refers to the historical practice of firing cannons or "heavy shot" into the air to attempt to disperse fog or storm clouds, a mechanical intervention the author deems futile.</note> might indeed scatter these clouds and this mist in one place or another for a short time, changing their form—but they do not clear them from the path, nor do they annihilate them. If, however, an improvement of the soil itself takes place, then they vanish of their own accord.

> Nature confounds the Skeptics, and reason confounds the Dogmatists. — We have an inability to prove things, which is invincible to all Dogmatism. Pascal refers to the "Pyrrhonists" (extreme skeptics) and "Dogmatists" (those who claim certain knowledge) to show that humans are suspended between doubt and certainty.</note>

And so I maintain, and shall continue to maintain: We do not create or instruct ourselves; we are in no way *independent of experience* <note>original: "a priori." In philosophy, this refers to knowledge that exists before or independent of sensory experience. The author is arguing that humans are entirely shaped by their interactions with the world.</note>, and can know or do nothing *independent of experience*; we can experience nothing—without experience. We find ourselves placed upon this earth; and as our actions develop there, so too does our knowledge; as our moral character <note>original: "Beschaffenheit," referring to the essential state or constitution of one's being.</note> takes shape, so too does our insight into all things relating to it. When Sperthias and Bulis of Sparta went voluntarily to Susa <note>An ancient city in modern-day Iran, then the capital of the Persian Empire.</note> as if to their deaths, they came to Hydarnes, who was a Persian and placed in command over the peoples living by the sea in Asia. He offered them gifts, entertained them, and wanted to persuade them to become friends of his king, and to become as great and happy as he himself was. "Your advice," the men said, "is good according to your experience, but not according to ours. Had you tasted the happiness which we enjoy, you would advise us to surrender wealth and life <note>original: "Gut und Blut," a German idiom meaning one's entire property and physical life.</note> for it *)."

Without doubt, Hydarnes laughed at these enthusiasts <note>original: "Schwärmer." In the 18th century, this term was often used pejoratively for those perceived as irrational or fanatically idealistic.</note>, and who among our contemporaries will not laugh at them along with him. Suppose

<meta>This page continues the philosophical reflection on the encounter between the Spartan ambassadors and the Persian officials, shifting from the concept of subjective experience to the nature of "truth" and cultural values.</meta>

but what if we and Hydarnes were wrong, and those men from Sparta were not enthusiasts <note>original: "Schwärmer." In this period, the term referred to people caught up in irrational or fanatical idealism.</note>: would they not then be in possession of a truth that we lack? And would we not cease to laugh at them, if we ourselves became aware of this very truth?

Sperthias and Bulis did not say to Hydarnes: you are a fool, a man of weak spirit; rather, they admitted that he was wise in his own measure, discerning and good. Nor did they attempt to impart their truth to him; on the contrary, they explained how this could not be done.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Spartan ambassadors, contrasting their intuitive, history-bound loyalty with the more abstract reasoning of the Persians and French Enlightenment philosophers.</meta>

->( 183 )<-
->M 4<-

Sperthias and Bulis might easily have had less skill in thinking and drawing conclusions than the Persians. Nor did they rely on their intellect, on their refined judgment, but only on things, and on their inclination toward these things. They boasted of no virtue in doing so; they only confessed the disposition of their hearts, their passion <note>original: "Affect." In 18th-century German, this refers to a strong, driving emotion or state of mind rather than a fleeting feeling.</note>. They had no philosophy, or their philosophy was merely history.

And can a living philosophy ever be anything other than history? As the objects are, so are the perceptions; as the perceptions, so are the inclinations and passions; as the inclinations and passions, so are the actions; as the actions, so are the principles and the whole of knowledge. What gave the teachings of a Helvetius <note>Claude Adrien Helvétius (1715–1771), a French philosopher who argued that human behavior is shaped entirely by environment and sensation.</note> or a Diderot <note>Denis Diderot (1713–1784), the prominent Enlightenment thinker and editor of the *Encyclopédie*.</note> such rapid and general acceptance?

> “Why,” says <term>Epictetus</term> <note>original: "Epictet." Because, however wretched and worthless their chatter may be, they always speak according to their actual concepts and principles; whereas the fine things you bring forth merely come from your lips: Therefore, your speeches have neither strength nor life, and it is enough to make one yawn aloud to hear your admonitions and the pitiful <term>virtue</term> <note>original: "Tugend." --- Page 227 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that philosophy must be rooted in reality. While the previous page used the Stoic Epictetus to show that philosophy lacks power when it is just "lip service," this page expands that idea into a historical principle: that the "matter" of philosophy is history itself.</meta>

( 185 )

M 5

Philosophy cannot create its own substance; it is always found there in present or past history. We philosophize poorly based on past history if it contains experiences that we cannot repeat. We judge with reliability only that which lies before us. What lies before each age, it can observe, dissect, compare its parts with one another, order them, and trace them back to the simplest principles, making the correctness of these principles ever more distinct and striking, and their power ever more effective. And so every age has its own truth—whose substance corresponds to the substance of its experiences—just as it also has its own living philosophy, which represents the dominant <term>way of acting</term> <note>original: "Handlungsweise." This suggests that philosophy is the intellectual map of how people actually behave in a given era, rather than a set of rules they follow.</note> of that age in its progression.

From this, it would then follow: that the actions of human beings must not be derived so much from their philosophy, as their philosophy must be derived from their actions; that their history does not [emerge] from their <term>way of thinking</term> <note>original: "Denkungsart." A common 18th-century term (prominent in the works of Immanuel Kant) referring to a person’s fundamental mindset, character, or habitual way of processing thoughts.</note>—

<vocab>Philosophy, History, Age (Era), Experiences, Way of Acting, Way of Thinking</vocab>

<summary>The author argues that philosophy does not create its own content but instead reflects the lived reality and actions of its time. He posits a reversal of the traditional view, suggesting that human actions actually shape philosophy and history, rather than thoughts shaping actions.</summary>
<keywords>philosophy of history, lived experience, action and thought, principles, Denkungsart, historical observation</keywords>

--- Page 228 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 185, which posits that a society's philosophy is a reflection of its actions and history rather than the cause of them. It specifically addresses the relationship between morality and religion.</meta>

( 186 )

...spring [from their philosophy], but rather their <term>way of thinking</term> <note>original: "Denkungsart." As noted on the previous page, this refers to the fundamental mindset or intellectual character of a person or era.</note> [springs] from their history. It would be erroneous, for example, to explain the corrupted morals of the Romans at the time of the decline of their Republic as resulting from the <term>irreligion</term> <note>In this context, a disregard for traditional religious practices and the social order they supported.</note> taking hold at that time; on the contrary, the source of that pervasive irreligion must be sought in the corruption of morals. Just as the debauchery and unrestrained revelry of the contemporaries of an Ovid and Petronius, of a Catullus and Martial <note>Four Roman poets famous for their wit, but also for their explicit or satirical depictions of the hedonistic and often scandalous lifestyles of the Roman elite.</note>, should not be blamed on these poets—rather, these poets should be attributed to that very debauchery and unrestrained revelry. By this, I by no means deny that poets and philosophers, when they are themselves permeated by the spirit of their time, powerfully support this spirit. Human history is created by human beings, where one person contributes more and another less to its progress.

If, therefore, the philosophy—the way of thinking of an age—is to be improved, then its history, its way of acting, its way of life must first be improved, which—

<summary>The author argues that moral decay precedes and causes religious decline, using the Roman Republic as an example. *) “The outcome of the day is decided. Now pull the arrow from my wound,” said **Epaminondas**, “and let me bleed!” <note>Epaminondas (c. 418–362 BC) was a Theban general and statesman who transformed the Ancient Greek city-state of Thebes. He died at the Battle of Mantinea; according to legend, he refused to have the spearhead removed from his chest until he was assured of victory, knowing the removal would cause him to bleed to death.</note>

Under what circumstances, and through what kind of instruction, can such an admirable **character** be formed? Does one find it in the <term>nurseries</term> <note>original: "Pflanzschulen." Literally "planting schools," a metaphor for institutions that cultivate specific traits or social classes.</note> of **folly**, **wantonness**, and **vanity**, from which fashion and refined **social customs** spread? Does one find it in large, wealthy cities, where people compete with one another in **display and splendor**, and for the **glory** of a more considerable **fortune**? Or in the admired circles of the court, where one learns the art of smiling without contentment, of caressing without love, of wounding with the secret weapons of envy and **jealousy**; where one learns to ground one's personal importance upon things over which one cannot always be master with honor? — No; but rather in situations where the great feelings of the **heart** are awakened; where...

<meta>This page continues the philosophical reflection on the source of virtue and knowledge of the Divine, moving from the individual’s internal struggle to the collective history of a people.</meta>

<page-num>( 197 )</page-num>
<sig>N 3</sig>

Try to become perfect in any virtue; that is: to practice it purely and without ceasing. You will either have to give up, or else become as aware of God as you are aware of yourself. The former <note>referring to "giving up"</note> will happen if you bring nothing more than your own resolution to the task. For man is so imperfect and weak that he can neither find nor maintain his own law. His law today is his resolution today, and his resolution today is his desire today—a desire which his own will <note>The German "Willkühr" suggests a person’s subjective faculty of choice or arbitrary will.</note> could neither stir up nor is capable of making firm.

He must obey, trust, and keep his word and faith; he must not cultivate his own conceit <note>original: "Dünkel"</note> and seat it upon the throne: this is his first virtue, and it must be his last.

Just as the living philosophy—the mindset <note>original: "Denkungsart"</note>—of a people arises from its history or way of life, so too does its history or way of life arise from its origins, and from the institutions and laws that have emerged from them. All history passes into instruction and

Not from laws of reason or moving exhortations, but from instruction, demonstration, example, discipline <note>original: "Zucht"; this term carries a sense of upbringing, training, and orderly conduct.</note>, assistance, counsel and deed, service and command.

If the first human beings—emerging like sponges from the earth or as worms from the mud—did not come forth far more perfect than they are now born from the mother's womb (without the *oval hole* <note>original Latin: "foramen ovale"; an opening in the fetal heart. All constitutions proceed from a higher being; in their origin, all were theocratic <note>derived from "theocracy," a system of government where a deity is recognized as the supreme ruling authority.</note>. Complete submission to a higher authority <note>original: "höheres Ansehen"; referring here to the respect and standing of divine or sovereign laws.</note>, strict, holy obedience, has been the spirit of every era that brought forth great deeds, great sentiments, and great men in abundance. The holiest temple of the Spartans was dedicated to Fear <note>The Spartans worshipped Phobos (Fear), not as cowardice, but as the "awe" or "reverence" for the law that prevents citizens from committing shameful acts.</note>.

But who is the Lord, whose fear is wisdom, and from whose commandments light and life come?

Is he just any random person <note>original: "der erste der beste"; an idiom meaning the first one who comes along or someone chosen without consideration.</note>, and are we permitted only to grope blindly after him?

Blindly—if you are indeed blind! But are you truly so? And what was it that robbed you of all light? What moved you to exchange the teachings of your fathers for your own conceit <note>original: "Dünkel"; refers to an arrogant self-importance or intellectual pride.</note>? Did it happen to please the truth or the lie; did the spirit point the way for you, or your flesh, your will, or base desires?

I do not wish to press you or force a confession from you, through which I might say to you: Return to the place where you know well that your will became impure; where, out of disobedience rather than conscience, you transgressed the law under which you stood; where you abandoned the faith that was within you, and broke your word and your loyalty—Return, wash yourself, and follow the light once more, from...

<summary>The author argues that human behavior shapes our thoughts more than the reverse and that true knowledge is a spiritual mystery rather than a logical formula. He cites the theologian Jerusalem and the scientist Newton to illustrate that the divine will is the ultimate boundary of human philosophy.</summary>

...and movement originate from a will, that the power for it must proceed from a will, is a universal revelation—or a lie of nature. If it holds true in *any* case that *the voice of the people is the voice of God* <note>original: "vox populi, vox dei." A traditional proverb suggesting that truths held by the common people often reflect a higher divine truth.</note>, then it is certainly here. And so the raw savage errs less than the learned wiseacre <note>Klügling: A derogatory term used by the author for a person who possesses superficial intellectual cleverness but lacks deep, genuine wisdom. Often translated as "pseudo-intellectual" or "sophist."</note>. For the savage, however often he may confuse the external with the internal, mistake form for substance, or appearance for essence: he nevertheless knows of both, and does not err in the matter itself. The learned wiseacre, on the other hand, who recognizes only the external, takes appearance for substance and substance for appearance—he errs in the matter itself.

I do not know the nature of the will—that self-determining cause—nor its inner possibility and its laws. For I do not exist through myself. But I feel such a power as the innermost life of my existence <term>Daseyn: A philosophical term for "being" or "existence," emphasizing the state of being consciously present in the world.</term>; through it, I sense my origin, and in the use of this power, I learn what flesh and blood alone could not reveal to me. Upon this use, I find everything related

**Will, Revelation, Nature, The voice of the people is the voice of God, Savage, Wiseacre, Cause, Existence**

<summary>The author argues that the human will is the fundamental source of movement and power, a truth better understood by the "savage" than the over-intellectualized scholar. He suggests that while we cannot fully grasp the mechanics of the will, we experience it as our "innermost life" and the key to higher knowledge.</summary>

This is the glory of the Lord, the face of God, to which a mortal eye is unable to lift itself. But in His goodness He descends to us; through His grace the Eternal becomes present to man, and He speaks with him—to whom He gave breath <term>Othem: An archaic German form of *Atem* (breath), specifically evoking the "breath of life" from the Book of Genesis.</term> from His mouth—through feelings of his own life, of his own blessedness <term>Seeligkeit: A state of spiritual joy or heavenly bliss; often used in this period to describe the soul’s ultimate happiness in God.</term>— I fall silent, I sink down, glowing with gratitude and rapture. — It would be shameful if I could ask for a better path to knowledge <term>Erkenntniß: Deep spiritual realization or profound understanding that goes beyond mere factual learning.</term> and peace—whoever might know it, let him show it to me! — Oh, that I were strong and fast enough to run it, the one and only magnificent path of God's love, of God's blessedness!

--- Page 267 ---
<meta>This page is the title page for Moses Mendelssohn’s final major work, published in 1786. It follows the errata list of the preceding volume, marking the beginning of his public response to the "Pantheism Controversy" that embroiled the German Enlightenment in its final years.</meta>

# Moses Mendelssohn

->to<-

# the Friends of Lessing. <note>Gotthold Ephraim Lessing (1729–1781) was a renowned German playwright and philosopher. His close friendship with Mendelssohn was a symbol of interfaith cooperation during the Enlightenment.</note>

---

->An Appendix<-

->to<-

->Mr. Jacobi’s Correspondence<- <note>Friedrich Heinrich Jacobi (1743–1819) was a philosopher who sparked a major scandal by claiming that the deceased Lessing had been a "Spinozist," a term then synonymous with atheism.</note>

->concerning<-

->the Doctrine of Spinoza.<- <note>Baruch Spinoza (1632–1677) was a Dutch-Jewish philosopher whose system suggested that God and the Universe are one and the same—a view that contemporary critics viewed as a denial of a personal God.</note>

---

->Berlin, 1786.<-

->Published by Christian Friedrich Voß and Son.<-

<summary>This title page introduces Moses Mendelssohn's final defense of his late friend Gotthold Ephraim Lessing, published shortly after Mendelssohn's death in 1786. The work is a critical entry in the "Pantheism Controversy," a debate over the nature of God and the limits of reason that defined the end of the German Enlightenment.</summary>
<keywords>Moses Mendelssohn, Gotthold Ephraim Lessing, Friedrich Heinrich Jacobi, Baruch Spinoza, Pantheism Controversy, Enlightenment, Berlin, publishing history</keywords>
